</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>Agro-industrial byproducts from Colombian <em>Passiflora</em> species represent an underexplored source of chemically diverse metabolites with promising cosmetic and pharmaceutical potential. This study investigated the chemical profiles and photoprotective potential of polar extracts obtained from byproducts (leaves, pericarps, and seeds) of six commercially relevant <em>Passiflora</em> species cultivated in Colombia (<em>P. ligularis</em>, <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>edulis</em>, <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em>, <em>P. maliformis</em>, <em>P. quadrangularis</em> and <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em>). Butanolic fractions from leaves and pericarps and hydroethanolic seed extracts were analyzed using <sup>1</sup>H NMR, GC-FID, GC-MS and UHPLC-qTOF. NMR profiling revealed aromatic signals mainly associated with flavonoids and stilbenoids in leaves and pericarps, while seeds exhibited abundant fatty acids, particularly linoleic acid. Molecular networking enabled the visualization of chemical diversity and supported the identification of 74 metabolites, including flavonoids, saponins, and stilbenoids, using Global Natural Products Social Molecular Networking (GNPS), SIRIUS (Version 6.0.5) software, and comparison with the literature. In vitro spectrophotometric photoprotective evaluation using the Mansur equation at 200 ppm showed that leaf extracts exhibited the highest sun protection factor (SPF) values, followed by seeds and pericarps, consistent with their phenolic composition. All active extracts demonstrated broad-spectrum protection, with high UVA ratios and critical wavelength values. These findings highlight the potential of <em>Passiflora</em> byproducts as sustainable sources of natural photoprotective agents for cosmetic applications.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> <em>Passiflora</em>, byproducts, molecular networking, dereplication, GNPS, flavonoids, stilbenoids, saponins, photoprotection</p></section></section><section id="sec1-plants-15-00972"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>The genus <em>Passiflora</em> L. is widely recognized for its remarkable chemical and biological diversity. Previous studies conducted by our research group have significantly contributed to this field by profiling flavonoids and saponins in the polar extracts of leaves from select South American <em>Passiflora</em> species, identifying key chemical markers with potential pharmaceutical applications [<a href="#B1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-plants-15-00972">1</a>,<a href="#B2-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-plants-15-00972">2</a>,<a href="#B3-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-plants-15-00972">3</a>]. However, given the vast diversity of <em>Passiflora</em> species in Colombia and their agro-industrial significance, further research on Colombian Passiflora species remains essential [<a href="#B4-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-plants-15-00972">4</a>]. Moreover, such investigations should extend beyond leaves to include other plant parts, such as pericarps and seeds, to comprehensively assess their chemical composition and potential industrial applications [<a href="#B5-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5-plants-15-00972">5</a>,<a href="#B6-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6-plants-15-00972">6</a>].</p>
<p>In Colombia, several <em>Passiflora</em> species hold significant agro-industrial value, contributing to both domestic consumption and international export markets. These include yellow passion fruit or maracuyá (<em>Passiflora edulis</em> Sims var. <em>flavicarpa</em> Deg.), purple passion fruit or gulupa (<em>Passiflora edulis</em> Sims var. <em>edulis</em>), sweet granadilla or granadilla (<em>Passiflora ligularis</em> Juss), banana passion fruit or curuba (<em>Passiflora tarminiana</em> Coppens &amp; V.E. Barney × <em>Passiflora tripartita</em> (Juss.) Poir.), sweet calabash or cholupa (<em>Passiflora maliformis</em> L.), and giant granadilla or badea (<em>Passiflora quadrangularis</em> L.) [<a href="#B7-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-plants-15-00972">7</a>]. The production of these species generates considerable agro-industrial residues, primarily leaves, pericarps, and seeds, which account for approximately 60–70% of the total fruit weight [<a href="#B8-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-plants-15-00972">8</a>,<a href="#B9-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-plants-15-00972">9</a>]. While these byproducts are often discarded, they possess significant potential for applications in industries such as cosmetics and pharmaceuticals, where their chemical composition is essential in ensuring the safety and efficacy of final formulations [<a href="#B10-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-plants-15-00972">10</a>].</p>
<p>Given the potential of these byproducts, it is essential to characterize their chemical composition and evaluate their possible applications. While chemical composition data are available for extracts from the leaves and seeds of certain species—particularly <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>edulis</em> [<a href="#B11-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-plants-15-00972">11</a>,<a href="#B12-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-plants-15-00972">12</a>,<a href="#B13-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-plants-15-00972">13</a>,<a href="#B14-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-plants-15-00972">14</a>,<a href="#B15-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-plants-15-00972">15</a>,<a href="#B16-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-plants-15-00972">16</a>,<a href="#B17-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-plants-15-00972">17</a>] and <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em> [<a href="#B1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-plants-15-00972">1</a>,<a href="#B13-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-plants-15-00972">13</a>,<a href="#B17-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-plants-15-00972">17</a>,<a href="#B18-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-plants-15-00972">18</a>,<a href="#B19-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-plants-15-00972">19</a>,<a href="#B20-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-plants-15-00972">20</a>,<a href="#B21-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-plants-15-00972">21</a>,<a href="#B22-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-plants-15-00972">22</a>] and, to a lesser extent, for <em>P. ligularis</em> [<a href="#B23-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-plants-15-00972">23</a>,<a href="#B24-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-plants-15-00972">24</a>] and <em>P. quadrangularis</em> [<a href="#B2-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-plants-15-00972">2</a>,<a href="#B15-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-plants-15-00972">15</a>,<a href="#B25-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25-plants-15-00972">25</a>]—the characterization of pericarps remains limited. This knowledge gap is especially pronounced for <em>P. maliformis</em>, for which chemical profiling studies are scarce [<a href="#B26-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26-plants-15-00972">26</a>]. The lack of comprehensive data on these byproducts hinders their valorization and potential industrial applications [<a href="#B10-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-plants-15-00972">10</a>,<a href="#B27-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-plants-15-00972">27</a>]. Therefore, systematic chemical profiling approaches are necessary to bridge this gap and unlock the full potential of these agro-industrial residues.</p>
<p>Chemical profiling involves the detection, identification, and/or quantification of natural compounds and includes metabolic profiling, focused on specific compound groups; and metabolic fingerprinting, an untargeted approach that detects a broader range of metabolites without necessarily achieving full identification [<a href="#B28-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-plants-15-00972">28</a>,<a href="#B29-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-plants-15-00972">29</a>]. These strategies commonly rely on chromatographic, spectroscopic, and mass spectrometric techniques, including hyphenated platforms such as LC-PDA, GC-FID, LC-MS, and GC-MS [<a href="#B30-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-plants-15-00972">30</a>]. Within this framework, dereplication enables the recognition of previously reported metabolites based on spectroscopic and spectrometric data, allowing the analysis of complex mixtures without compound isolation [<a href="#B28-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-plants-15-00972">28</a>,<a href="#B31-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-plants-15-00972">31</a>]. Recent computational tools such as Global Natural Products Social Molecular Networking (GNPS) and SIRIUS have further improved dereplication workflows by facilitating the interpretation of large-scale mass spectrometric datasets and supporting molecular formula prediction, fragmentation analysis, and chemical classification [<a href="#B32-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-plants-15-00972">32</a>,<a href="#B33-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-plants-15-00972">33</a>,<a href="#B34-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-plants-15-00972">34</a>,<a href="#B35-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-plants-15-00972">35</a>,<a href="#B36-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-plants-15-00972">36</a>].</p>
<p>In recent years, the search for natural photoprotective compounds has gained increasing attention due to the adverse effects associated with solar radiation, including sunburn, skin cancer, and photoaging [<a href="#B28-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-plants-15-00972">28</a>,<a href="#B29-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-plants-15-00972">29</a>,<a href="#B30-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-plants-15-00972">30</a>]. Moreover, there is a growing interest in replacing conventional synthetic sunscreens with safer and more environmentally sustainable alternatives [<a href="#B31-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-plants-15-00972">31</a>]. In this context, agro-industrial byproducts, such as the residues generated from the cultivation of commercial <em>Passiflora</em> species in Colombia, represent a promising source of bioactive molecules for cosmetic applications, aligned with the upcycling concept [<a href="#B32-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-plants-15-00972">32</a>,<a href="#B33-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-plants-15-00972">33</a>].</p>
<p>In this context, the present study aimed to phytochemically characterize the extracts derived from byproducts—including leaves, seeds, and pericarps—of the principal commercial <em>Passiflora</em> species in Colombia (<em>P. ligularis</em>, <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>edulis</em>, <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em>, <em>P. quadrangularis</em>, <em>P. maliformis</em>, and <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em>) using metabolomics-based approaches and to evaluate their photoprotective potential.</p></section><section id="sec2-plants-15-00972"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Results and Discussion</h2>
<p>The extraction methods employed were selected based on previous studies, which have shown that butanolic fractions (BFs) allow the recovery of the main compounds present in <em>Passiflora</em> genus (flavonoids, saponins, phenolic compounds) from leaves and pericarps [<a href="#B15-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-plants-15-00972">15</a>,<a href="#B34-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-plants-15-00972">34</a>,<a href="#B35-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-plants-15-00972">35</a>]. For the seeds, hydroethanolic extracts (HEs) have been reported for some <em>Passiflora</em> species, enabling the identification of various types of compounds, such as stilbenes [<a href="#B22-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-plants-15-00972">22</a>]. The corresponding extraction yields (%) of BFs and HEs are presented in <a href="#app1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link">Table S1</a>.</p>
<section id="sec2dot1-plants-15-00972"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. <sup>1</sup>H NMR Preliminary Profiling</h3>
<p>The <sup>1</sup>H NMR spectra of the BFs obtained from the leaves (<a href="#plants-15-00972-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>) revealed three main signal regions. Characteristic signals corresponding to the aromatic protons (δ<sub>H</sub> 6.0–8.0) were detected in all evaluated extracts, with higher intensity in those from <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em>, <em>P. maliformis</em>, and <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em>. Additionally, signals corresponding to carbinolic protons (δ<sub>H</sub> 3.5–5.5) were observed. Finally, intense signals were identified in the δ<sub>H</sub> 0.75–1.25 ppm region, which were more abundant in species such as <em>P. ligularis</em>, <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>edulis</em>, and <em>P. quadrangularis</em>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="plants-15-00972-f001"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=13030438_plants-15-00972-g001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/803d/13030438/027882347705/plants-15-00972-g001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="492" width="766" alt="Figure 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/plants-15-00972-f001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><sup>1</sup>H NMR spectra of the evaluated <em>Passiflora</em> BFs from leaves in MD6.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The aromatic signals in the δ<sub>H</sub> 6.0–8.0 region are characteristic of flavonoids. Previous studies have reported the presence of C-glycosylated flavonoids in <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em> [<a href="#B1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-plants-15-00972">1</a>,<a href="#B19-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-plants-15-00972">19</a>,<a href="#B20-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-plants-15-00972">20</a>,<a href="#B36-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-plants-15-00972">36</a>,<a href="#B37-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-plants-15-00972">37</a>] and <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em> [<a href="#B38-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-plants-15-00972">38</a>]. However, for <em>P. maliformis</em>, which also exhibited significant signal abundance in this region, no studies on leaf composition have been reported to date; only preliminary analyses of seeds and pericarps are available [<a href="#B39-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-plants-15-00972">39</a>]. The signals in the δ<sub>H</sub> 3.5–5.5 region are likely associated with the sugar moieties of glycosylated flavonoids, compounds characteristic of this genus [<a href="#B1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-plants-15-00972">1</a>,<a href="#B2-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-plants-15-00972">2</a>,<a href="#B23-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-plants-15-00972">23</a>,<a href="#B38-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-plants-15-00972">38</a>]. Likewise, the intense signals observed in the δ<sub>H</sub> 0.75–1.25 ppm region are characteristic of methyl groups in saponin-type compounds [<a href="#B40-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-plants-15-00972">40</a>,<a href="#B41-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-plants-15-00972">41</a>], aligning with previous reports on leaf extracts of <em>P. ligularis</em>, <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>edulis</em>, and <em>P. quadrangularis</em> [<a href="#B15-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-plants-15-00972">15</a>,<a href="#B23-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-plants-15-00972">23</a>,<a href="#B42-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-plants-15-00972">42</a>].</p>
<p>For the butanolic fractions (BFs) of the pericarps, the <sup>1</sup>H NMR spectra predominantly exhibited signals between δ<sub>H</sub> 3.0 and 4.5, while signals in the aromatic region (δ<sub>H</sub> 6–8) were less prominent. The predominance of signals between δ<sub>H</sub> 3.0 and 4.5 corresponds to a region typically associated with sugars and carbohydrates, which aligns with the high abundance of these compounds in this part of the fruit (<a href="#app1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link">Figure S1</a>) [<a href="#B39-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-plants-15-00972">39</a>,<a href="#B43-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-plants-15-00972">43</a>,<a href="#B44-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-plants-15-00972">44</a>]. The lower intensity of aromatic signals suggests the presence of aromatic compounds similar to those detected in the leaves, albeit at lower concentrations. Previous studies have reported the presence of flavonoids in the pericarps of several <em>Passiflora</em> species, including <em>P. ligularis</em> [<a href="#B45-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-plants-15-00972">45</a>], <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>edulis</em> [<a href="#B45-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-plants-15-00972">45</a>], <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em> [<a href="#B46-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-plants-15-00972">46</a>], <em>P. maliformis</em> [<a href="#B39-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-plants-15-00972">39</a>] and <em>P. tripartita</em> var. <em>mollisima</em> [<a href="#B45-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-plants-15-00972">45</a>].</p>
<p>The <sup>1</sup>H NMR spectra (<a href="#app1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link">Figure S2</a>) indicate that the HEs of seeds exhibit signals at δ<sub>H</sub> 2.3, 1.6, 1.29, and 1.35, as well as signals at 0.98 ppm and around δ<sub>H</sub> 5.3. Additional signals were observed at δ<sub>H</sub> 2.80 and 2.07. The fatty acid profile appears to be highly similar across all species. Additionally, the HEs exhibit signals in the carbohydrate region (δ<sub>H</sub> 3.0–4.5) and in the aromatic region (δ<sub>H</sub> 6.0–7.5), with the most distinct spectrum in the aromatic region corresponding to <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em>, although it still exhibits the characteristic signals observed in the other species.</p>
<p>The signals at δ<sub>H</sub> 2.3, 1.6, 1.29, and 1.35 correspond to characteristic methylene groups of fatty acids [<a href="#B47-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-plants-15-00972">47</a>]. The signal at 0.98 ppm suggests the occurrence of saturated fatty acids, while olefinic proton signals around δ<sub>H</sub> 5.3 indicate the presence of double bonds, further supported by signals at δ<sub>H</sub> 2.80 and 2.07 corresponding to allylic protons [<a href="#B42-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-plants-15-00972">42</a>]. Given the polarity of the hydroethanolic extract, the presence of glycolipids is more likely than that of free fatty acids or monosaccharides. Moreover, the aromatic signals in the δ<sub>H</sub> 6.0–7.5 region are more consistent with stilbenes than with flavonoids, as the latter typically resonate at lower field. This interpretation is consistent with previous reports of stilbenes such as piceatannol and its derivatives in <em>Passiflora</em> species, including <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em> and <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>edulis</em> [<a href="#B11-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-plants-15-00972">11</a>,<a href="#B13-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-plants-15-00972">13</a>,<a href="#B22-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-plants-15-00972">22</a>,<a href="#B48-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-plants-15-00972">48</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot2-plants-15-00972"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. GC-FID FAMEs Analysis</h3>
<p>As indicated by the preliminary profiling performed via NMR and supported by previous studies, seeds from <em>Passiflora</em> species are known to contain high levels of fatty acids [<a href="#B49-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-plants-15-00972">49</a>]. To further characterize these fatty acids, gas chromatography with flame ionization detection (GC-FID) and gas chromatography with mass spectrometry (GC-MS) were employed. The chromatograms of fatty acids for each species are presented in <a href="#app1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link">Figure S3</a>.</p>
<p>The seed extracts of the evaluated <em>Passiflora</em> species exhibited low fatty acid diversity, with a largely conserved profile across species, except for <em>P. quadrangularis</em>, where two additional signals were detected, as detailed in <a href="#plants-15-00972-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#app1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link">Table S2</a>. Linoleic acid was identified as the most abundant fatty acid in all species, comprising over 50% of the total fatty acids, followed by oleic and palmitic acids, while stearic acid was detected as a minor component.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="plants-15-00972-t001"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 1.</h4>
<div class="caption p"><p>Relative abundance of Fatty acids identified in the FAHOs evaluated <em>Passiflora</em> seeds, reported as methyl ester form (FAMEs).</p></div>
<div class="tbl-box p" tabindex="0"><table class="content" frame="hsides" rules="groups">
<thead><tr>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/plants-15-00972-t001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn2"><p><sup>1</sup> Proposed by GC-MS analysis.</p></div></div></section><p>This conserved fatty acid profile aligns with previously reported studies, which have extensively characterized the lipid composition of <em>Passiflora</em> seeds and confirmed the predominance of linoleic, oleic, palmitic, and stearic acids in these species [<a href="#B12-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-plants-15-00972">12</a>,<a href="#B49-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-plants-15-00972">49</a>,<a href="#B50-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-plants-15-00972">50</a>,<a href="#B51-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-plants-15-00972">51</a>,<a href="#B52-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-plants-15-00972">52</a>,<a href="#B53-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-plants-15-00972">53</a>,<a href="#B54-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-plants-15-00972">54</a>].</p>
<p>In the case of <em>P. quadrangularis</em> seeds, myristic acid was uniquely identified, along with an additional signal observed between the retention times of myristic and palmitic acid. GC-MS analysis revealed a molecular ion [M<sup>+</sup>] with <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 268.18 for this peak. Based on the obtained MS/MS fragmentation pattern and comparison with NIST library data, showing agreement with methyl palmitoleate, this compound was tentatively annotated as palmitoleic acid. The corresponding fragmentation spectrum is provided in <a href="#app1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link">Figure S4</a>. This fatty acid has previously been reported at low concentrations in <em>P. edulis</em> seed extracts [<a href="#B55-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-plants-15-00972">55</a>,<a href="#B56-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-plants-15-00972">56</a>], supporting the plausibility of its occurrence in <em>P. quadrangularis</em>.</p></section><section id="sec2dot3-plants-15-00972"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. UHPLC-MS/MS Profiling</h3>
<section id="sec2dot3dot1-plants-15-00972"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.1. Molecular Networking and Compound Classification</h4>
<p>Molecular networks provide a graphical representation of the chemical space within analyzed extracts, enabling comparative analysis between samples and the identification of structural relationships among detected compounds [<a href="#B57-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-plants-15-00972">57</a>]. <a href="#app1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link">Figure S5a</a> illustrates the most representative clusters identified in negative ionization mode for the leaf BFs of the evaluated <em>Passiflora</em> species, with the ClassyFire chemical classification assigned by GNPS. This ionization mode facilitates the detection of more abundant phenolic compounds [<a href="#B58-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-plants-15-00972">58</a>]. The largest clusters corresponded to flavonoids (clusters 1, 2, 5, 6, 7, and 16), followed by saponins (cluster 3) and fatty acids (clusters 4 and 27). Additionally, minor compounds were identified, including purine nucleosides (cluster 8), anthraquinones (cluster 9), growth hormones and regulators such as jasmonic acid derivatives, indole-3-acetic acid, and phenylimidazoles (clusters 12, 17, and 15), as well as alkaloid and carbazole derivatives (clusters 13 and 11).</p>
<p>The predominance of flavonoid clusters in negative ionization mode is consistent with the known ionization behavior of phenolic compounds and aligns with previous reports on the chemical composition of <em>Passiflora</em> leaves [<a href="#B15-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-plants-15-00972">15</a>,<a href="#B23-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-plants-15-00972">23</a>]. The detection of minor compound classes such as anthraquinones, alkaloids, and plant growth regulators further supports the chemical diversity previously described for this genus [<a href="#B59-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-plants-15-00972">59</a>,<a href="#B60-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-plants-15-00972">60</a>,<a href="#B61-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-plants-15-00972">61</a>].</p>
<p>Among the flavonoid clusters, <em>P. ligularis</em> was predominantly enriched in flavonoid-3-<em>O</em>-glycosides (cluster 1), whereas <em>P. maliformis</em>, <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em>, and <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em> showed higher abundance of flavonoid-7-<em>O</em>-glycosides (cluster 2) and flavonoid-8-<em>C</em>-glycosides (cluster 6). Regarding saponins (cluster 3), they were present in almost all species except <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em>, while fatty acid derivatives (cluster 4) were found across all evaluated species.</p>
<p>This species-dependent distribution of flavonoid subclasses is consistent with previous phytochemical studies on these taxa [<a href="#B2-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-plants-15-00972">2</a>,<a href="#B23-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-plants-15-00972">23</a>,<a href="#B24-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-plants-15-00972">24</a>,<a href="#B38-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-plants-15-00972">38</a>] and may reflect differences in glycosylation patterns within the genus. The widespread presence of fatty acid derivatives and saponins also agrees with earlier compositional analyses of <em>Passiflora</em> leaves [<a href="#B15-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-plants-15-00972">15</a>,<a href="#B23-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-plants-15-00972">23</a>].</p>
<p>The leaf BFs were further analyzed by UHPLC-MS/MS in positive ionization mode to enhance the characterization of saponins, as suggested by UHPLC-DAD-ELSD analyses. The resulting molecular network (<a href="#app1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link">Figure S5b</a>) provided complementary information to the negative ionization data, facilitating the dereplication process. In this case, the most abundant clusters corresponded to steroidal glycosides (clusters 1 and 2), with chemical classification assigned by the CANOPUS tool in SIRIUS. Flavonoids (clusters 3 and 11) were also detected in this mode. Additionally, the positive ionization network revealed dipeptides (cluster 8), terpenes (clusters 6, 7, and 10), and esters (cluster 17), which were not prominently detected in negative mode.</p>
<p>The detection of steroidal glycosides predominantly in positive ionization mode is consistent with their enhanced ionization efficiency under these conditions. The complementary nature of both ionization modes highlights the importance of dual-mode UHPLC-MS/MS analysis for comprehensive metabolite coverage, particularly for compound classes with different ionization behaviors.</p>
<p>The molecular networks of the primary clusters identified in the BFs of the pericarps (<a href="#app1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link">Figure S6</a>) revealed a diverse array of compounds. The most prominent clusters included <em>O</em>-glycosides, glycosylated phenolics, and flavonoid-3-<em>O</em>-glycosides (clusters 1, 2, and 3), which were widely distributed among species, particularly in <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>edulis</em>, <em>P. ligularis</em>, <em>P. maliformis</em>, and <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em>. Other notable clusters comprised long-chain fatty acids (cluster 4), xanthones and purine bases (cluster 8), butenolides (cluster 5), and triterpenoids (cluster 7). Additionally, glycosylated compounds (cluster 13) were observed, though they were not exclusive to any particular species. Some clusters were species-specific, such as glycosylated iridoids and hybrid peptides, which were uniquely detected in <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>edulis</em>.</p>
<p>Although a broad range of compound classes was detected, most were present in minor quantities, which is consistent with the limited chemical diversity observed during the preliminary profiling. The predominance of glycosylated phenolics and flavonoid derivatives in the major clusters agrees with previous reports describing these compounds as characteristic constituents of <em>Passiflora</em> pericarps. Furthermore, the occurrence of species-specific clusters, such as glycosylated iridoids and hybrid peptides in <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>edulis</em>, suggests potential chemotaxonomic differences within the evaluated species.</p>
<p>The primary clusters identified in the seed HE networks (<a href="#app1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link">Figure S7</a>) highlight stilbenoids (cluster 1) as a group of compounds present in the seeds of all evaluated species except <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em>. Other clusters, such as glycosylated phenolics (cluster 2), 2-arylbenzofuran flavonoids (cluster 3), and terpenes (cluster 4), were detected in most extracts. Notably, cluster 3 exhibited greater diversity in <em>P. quadrangularis</em>. Some clusters were species-specific, such as alkyl-phenyl-ketones (cluster 7) and 2′-dihydrochalcones (cluster 16), which were only found in <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em>.</p>
<p>The widespread presence of stilbenoids across species, except for <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em>, supports previous reports describing these compounds as characteristic constituents of <em>Passiflora</em> seeds. The higher diversity observed in cluster 3 for <em>P. quadrangularis</em> suggests a broader structural variability of 2-arylbenzofuran flavonoids in this species. Likewise, the occurrence of species-specific clusters in <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em> may indicate distinctive metabolic features that differentiate this taxon from the other evaluated species.</p></section><section id="sec2dot3dot2-plants-15-00972"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.2. Dereplication of Butanolic Extracts of Pericarps and Leaves of Commercial <em>Passiflora</em> Species</h4>
<p>The UHPLC-DAD-ELSD profiles of the extracts were used to identify major compounds and establish correlations among the detected metabolites. The most abundant peaks in each evaluated extract were dereplicated using tools provided by GNPS and SIRIUS. Additionally, retention time data—specifically elution order—and UV absorption maxima (where applicable) were utilized to associate detected compounds with those previously reported in the literature or in studies from our research group. This step was essential, as some compounds have been documented in scientific reports but are not included in mass spectrometry databases. Consequently, such metabolites cannot be dereplicated solely through database searches.</p>
<p>For the compound dereplication process, the data were grouped by plant part (leaves, pericarps, and seeds). This decision was based on information provided by the previously mentioned tools (UHPLC-DAD-ELSD, <sup>1</sup>H NMR, molecular networking), which demonstrated a greater similarity and association among compounds derived from the same plant part, regardless of species. This grouping facilitated the identification of potential common compounds across species.</p>
<p>When using database search engines such as GNPS and SIRIUS, it is crucial to integrate multiple tools to cross-check information and enhance reliability [<a href="#B59-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-plants-15-00972">59</a>]. Additionally, the similarity scores provided by these search engines must be carefully considered. For GNPS, the cosine score is used as a mathematical measure of similarity between two fragmentation spectra (experimental and database-reported), ranging from 1 (identical fragmentation) to 0 (no similarity) [<a href="#B60-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-plants-15-00972">60</a>]. For SIRIUS, the CSI:FingerID score was employed. This software predicts a molecular fingerprint for the unknown compound based on the MS/MS-derived fragmentation tree and compares it to structural fingerprints of candidate molecules retrieved from databases using a kernel-based machine learning approach. The resulting score corresponds to a log-likelihood value ranging from −∞ to 0; therefore, values closer to 0 indicate a better probabilistic agreement between the predicted and database fingerprints, rather than a conventional similarity coefficient [<a href="#B59-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-plants-15-00972">59</a>,<a href="#B61-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-plants-15-00972">61</a>]. For compounds identified exclusively through SIRIUS/CSI:FingerID, only candidates with a confidence score above 0.5 were considered for putative structural annotation (Schymanski Level 2) [<a href="#B62-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B62-plants-15-00972">62</a>].</p>
<p><a href="#plants-15-00972-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a> displays the chromatograms of the BFs from the leaves of the evaluated species, with peak numbering assigned based on retention time or elution order. The numbering for BFs from leaves and pericarps was grouped to account for compounds detected in both plant parts. <a href="#plants-15-00972-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a> provides a comprehensive summary of the identified compounds in the BFs from leaves and pericarps, including their names, molecular ion <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> values ([M − H]<sup>−</sup> or [M + H]<sup>+</sup> where applicable), retention times, major fragment <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> values, UV <em>λmax</em> values (for aromatic compounds with UV absorption), the dereplication tools used (GNPS, SIRIUS, the literature, or their combination), compound type, chemical classification based on GNPS and CANOPUS (SIRIUS), mass error (ppm) relative to the exact mass reported in the literature (if identified), and similarity scores from search engines (cosine score and CSI:FingerID score).</p>

<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn3"><p>* Pseudomolecular ion detected in positive ionization mode [M + H]. <sup>1</sup> UHPLC-MS/MS retention time. <sup>2</sup> Source of dereplication information used (identification, chemical classification). <sup>3</sup> Chemical classification given by MolNetEnhacer (GNPS) in two levels, class and parent. <sup>4</sup> Chemical classification given by CANOPUS (SIRIUS). This was taken at the most specific level determined. <sup>5</sup> Similarity factor generated by GNPS, ranging from 0 to 1, where values close to 1 indicate a higher similarity between the fragmentation patterns and the compound reported in the literature. <sup>6</sup> Similarity score generated by SIRIUS, for which values closer to 0 indicate a better match between the fragmentation patterns. <sup>7</sup> L: Leaves; P: Pericarps. <sup>8</sup> Dereplication level based on the criteria established by [<a href="#B64-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B64-plants-15-00972">64</a>,<a href="#B65-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65-plants-15-00972">65</a>].</p></div></div></section><p>By integrating multiple analytical approaches, we identified a total of 74 major compounds across the 12 analyzed extracts from leaves and pericarps (<a href="#plants-15-00972-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). The dereplication process involved assessing the confidence level of metabolite identification based on the characterization levels proposed by Schymanski and Schrimpe-Rutledge for small molecule identification using HRMS data [<a href="#B62-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B62-plants-15-00972">62</a>,<a href="#B63-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63-plants-15-00972">63</a>]. Some compounds were classified as probable structure (Level 2), while others were classified at Level 3 based on chemical family, parent compound, or CANOPUS-based classification, primarily relying on fragmentation patterns. In certain cases, compounds were associated with known metabolites from the <em>Passiflora</em> genus based on shared molecular ions (Level 3). In less conclusive instances, only the molecular ion was reported (Level 4) due to the absence of comparative literature data. It is important to emphasize that dereplication was performed on complex mixtures, without isolation or structural elucidation of novel compounds. Consequently, the highest level of dereplication achieved through HRMS-based methods was Level 2.</p>
<p>This table highlights that flavonoids are the predominant compounds in the BFs from leaves, with glycosylated derivatives being the most abundant (31 compounds), of which 26 were identified. These are followed by saponins (13 compounds), with 10 identified, as inferred from the previously discussed molecular networks. It is important to note that while the flavonoids are present across the evaluated species and saponins in some of them, their specific chemical profiles exhibit considerable variation.</p>
<p>The identified flavonoids display significant structural diversity, with few occurring in only two species. For example, vitexin (10) was detected in the BFs from the leaves of <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em>, where it had been previously reported [<a href="#B1-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-plants-15-00972">1</a>], as well as in <em>P. maliformis</em>, where it was the most abundant compound despite the lack of prior documentation, likely due to the limited number of studies on this species. Conversely, the identified saponins were species-specific, with distinct profiles varying significantly among the evaluated species.</p>
<p>The species <em>P. ligularis</em>, <em>P. quadrangularis</em>, <em>and P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em> were among those with the highest dereplication success, as they have been extensively studied [<a href="#B15-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-plants-15-00972">15</a>,<a href="#B23-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-plants-15-00972">23</a>,<a href="#B38-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-plants-15-00972">38</a>,<a href="#B63-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63-plants-15-00972">63</a>]. Notably, <em>P. ligularis</em> predominantly contains <em>O</em>-glycosylated flavonoids, distinguishing it from the other species, where <em>C</em>-glycosylated flavonoids prevail. For example, <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em> exhibits a wide variety of <em>C</em>-glycosylated flavonoids. In contrast, <em>P. maliformis</em> is the least studied species and chemically simpler, as evidenced by fewer peaks in the DAD chromatograms. Some compounds, such as diglycosylated flavonoids (e.g., saponarin (2)) and glycosylated flavonoids (<em>C</em>-glycosylated and 7-<em>O</em>-glycosylated), were dereplicated in this species based on reports from other evaluated species, such as vitexin (10) in <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em>.</p>
<p>For the BFs from the leaves of <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em>, the extracts were rich in aromatic compounds, and some of their most abundant constituents were identified. However, lower-abundance compounds, as suggested by ELSD data, could not be identified due to insufficient MS/MS spectral quality. Additionally, the literature often lacks clarity regarding the variety of <em>P. edulis</em> evaluated, complicating the differentiation between the <em>flavicarpa</em> (yellow passion fruit) and <em>edulis</em> (purple passion fruit) varieties [<a href="#B36-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-plants-15-00972">36</a>,<a href="#B37-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-plants-15-00972">37</a>]. Consequently, reported compounds may not always be distinctly attributed to one variety or the other. Notably, <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em> does not contain abundant saponins, whereas <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>edulis</em> has.</p>
<p>The presence of saponin-type compounds was confirmed in <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>edulis</em>, <em>P. ligularis</em>, and <em>P. quadrangularis</em>, with dereplication based on comparisons with previously reported data [<a href="#B15-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-plants-15-00972">15</a>,<a href="#B23-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-plants-15-00972">23</a>]. Interestingly, none of these compounds were dereplicated using the employed search engines, suggesting that while they are documented in the literature, they are not included in the databases integrated into these tools, thereby complicating their dereplication. Furthermore, classifications generated by GNPS and SIRIUS suggested the presence of steroidal glycosides (saponins) that have not been previously documented for the studied species. These saponins were assigned to dereplication Level 3 and represent minor components that require more comprehensive chemical investigations with larger sample quantities.</p>
<p>The BFs from pericarps were also analyzed using UHPLC-MS/MS. Considering the lower compound abundance compared to leaf extracts, the number of dereplicated compounds was also lower, with a total of 24 compounds identified, of which 16 were confidently assigned. It is important to note that pericarps remain one of the least studied plant parts of <em>Passiflora</em> species in terms of chemical composition. These harvest byproduct have primarily been utilized for the extraction of polymeric compounds such as pectin, a thickening agent widely used in various industries [<a href="#B43-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-plants-15-00972">43</a>,<a href="#B44-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-plants-15-00972">44</a>,<a href="#B66-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66-plants-15-00972">66</a>], and for producing flour from the pericarp of <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em> for applications in the food industry [<a href="#B67-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B67-plants-15-00972">67</a>]. The molecular networks of these pericarp extracts revealed the presence of glycosylated phenolics, particularly glycosylated flavonoids, which were found in relatively low abundance in these extracts. Notably, no saponins were identified in any of the studied species, in contrast to leaf extracts, where these compounds were detected and identified.</p>
<p><a href="#plants-15-00972-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a> presents the chromatograms of the BFs from pericarps, with identified compounds labeled and listed in <a href="#plants-15-00972-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>, alongside the dereplicated compounds from leaf BFs. Interestingly, some dereplicated compounds from the pericarp BFs of <em>P. ligularis</em> correspond to 3-<em>O</em>-glycosylated flavonoids, a compound type that also predominates in the leaf BFs of this species. Indeed, the species with the highest compound identification rates were those with the greatest abundance and diversity of flavonoids in their leaves, such as <em>P. ligularis</em>, <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em>, and <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em>. Several of these flavonoids were also detected in their pericarp BFs, consistent with previous reports [<a href="#B45-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-plants-15-00972">45</a>]. Furthermore, several dereplicated compounds in these three species, such as isoorientin (4) and isovitexin (14), have already been documented in polar extracts from <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em> pericarps [<a href="#B45-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-plants-15-00972">45</a>,<a href="#B46-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-plants-15-00972">46</a>,<a href="#B68-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B68-plants-15-00972">68</a>].</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="plants-15-00972-f003"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=13030438_plants-15-00972-g003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/803d/13030438/8ea0a01d3819/plants-15-00972-g003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="441" width="784" alt="Figure 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/plants-15-00972-f003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Pericarp BF chromatograms, recorded at 340 nm and using an ELSD detector. Species analyzed: (<strong>a</strong>) <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>flavicarpa</em>; (<strong>b</strong>) <em>P. tarminiana</em> × <em>P. tripartita</em>; (<strong>c</strong>) <em>P. maliformis</em>; (<strong>d</strong>) <em>P. quadrangularis</em>; (<strong>e</strong>) <em>P. edulis</em> var. <em>edulis</em>; (<strong>f</strong><em>) P. ligularis</em>. †: Signal attributed to noise; *: Signal present in the blank.</p></figcaption></figure><p>In the BFs from <em>P. maliformis</em> pericarps, the identified compounds differed from those found in leaf extracts (Saponarin, Apigenin-6-<em>C</em>-arabinosyl-7-<em>O</em>-glucoside, Vitexin). These compounds have not been previously reported in the literature for the pericarps of this species. However, the detection of several flavonoids is consistent with previous preliminary phytochemical screenings [<a href="#B39-plants-15-00972" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-plants-15-00972">39</a>], which reported the presence of flavonoids and other phenolic compounds. Although the dereplication rate for this species was lower compared to the other evaluated species, some flavonoids were still detected and/or tentatively identified, including schaftoside/corymboside (<strong>3</strong>).</p></section><section id="sec2dot3dot3-plants-15-00972"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.3. Dereplication of Major Compounds in Seeds of Commercial <em>Passiflora</em> Species</h4>
<p>The chromatograms of the HEs from the evaluated seeds are presented in <a href="#plants-15-00972-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>. Notably, the extracts exhibit high similarity, indicating that the detected compounds are largely shared among the species. The dereplication of abundant compounds was highly effective, resulting in the identification of 22 compounds across the six evaluated species, as detailed in <a href="#plants-15-00972-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>.</p>
